Research 08 §9 names `SIGSTOP` and clock skew as the rows that find real bugs, and both are untestable in-process. ⚠️ **EVERY ROW ASSERTS AN INVARIANT, NEVER A TIMING**, except the two criteria explicitly about time, which state a bound with headroom and report the measured number -- a flaky chaos row read as a finding is this milestone's named risk | NFR-8 | done — `server/src/integrationTest/.../chaos/`: `NodeProcess` starts `Main` in its own JVM (the test classpath, output to a file, MinIO credentials in the environment) and signals it with `kill()` (SIGKILL), `terminate()` (SIGTERM), and `pause()`/`resume()` (SIGSTOP/SIGCONT through `kill(1)`, exit status checked). `ChaosBucket` is a fresh MinIO bucket per row, read by a client that shares nothing with the nodes. `ChaosHarnessIT` asserts each verb by its effect on the lease at the store: a kill exits 137 and leaves the term held, a terminate releases it, and a 7 s pause freezes renewal, which resumes on SIGCONT. The network half (partition, skew) is M8.23 |
| M8.9 | **KILL MID-FLUSH: RPO 0 (NFR-8).** `SIGKILL` during a flush with >=1,000 acked records outstanding; every acked record readable afterwards. ⚠️ **DUPLICATES ARE PERMITTED AND ASSERTED AS SUCH** -- at-least-once with `_id` dedup is the contract (research 08 F2), and a criterion that forbids them is testing the wrong thing. ⚠️ **THE MUTATION IS ACKING BEFORE THE PUT COMPLETES**, which is invisible to every in-process test because the records are still in the heap that answers the read | NFR-8 | done — `KillMidFlushIT`: 8 producers write to a real process on MinIO, and once 1,000 or more records are acked a SIGKILL is sent. The kill is synchronised to an event in one of two ways. `AT_THE_ACK`: the producer kills the node the instant it sees an ack. That is where an ack that ran ahead of its PUT is exposed, and the ack-before-PUT mutation reds there with acked ids missing. `AT_THE_PUT`: the producer sends one more batch and kills the node the moment a new segment appears in the bucket. ⚠️ **THE WINDOW IS MEASURED AT THE STORE, AFTER THE KILL**: a run landed inside a flush if the bucket holds records that were never acked, meaning PUT done and ack not yet. That was 4 of 4, and at least 1 is asserted. Every acked id is in the bucket after every kill. Duplicates are allowed but bounded at 2 per id, since no producer retries. ⚠️ For a correct node the literal ack-then-PUT window is empty, so no `AT_THE_ACK` run is asserted to land in it |
| M8.10 | **KILL AFTER PUT, BEFORE COMMIT: THE ORPHAN IS SWEPT, THE RECORDS ARE RE-INGESTED, AND NO OFFSET GAP APPEARS** (research 08 F3). The segment PUT but never committed is deleted after `orphanGrace` BY THE ASSEMBLED SWEEP, the producer's retry lands the records in a NEW segment, and the consumer sees a contiguous offset sequence. ⚠️ **THE MUTATION IS A SWEEP THAT DELETES THE ORPHAN BEFORE THE GRACE PERIOD**, taking the retry's records with it -- which M7.8's within-grace KEEP case pins in-process and which this row pins against a real kill | NFR-8, FR-9 | blocked by M8.42 — FOUND BY THIS ROW (M8.11's session): an orphan left by a kill is written BEFORE its successor's term began, and the assembled sweep only enters hours starting `RetentionLoop.SKEW_MARGIN` (1 h) after the term was first seen. So NO term ever sweeps a crash's orphan, and this row's first clause cannot pass. M8.42 says the confinement must not be widened, because the keep list lacks the pre-checkpoint deltas and fails open. A keep list complete back to the retention floor is a design decision whose wrong answer deletes acknowledged data, so it is put to the user rather than made here |
| M8.11 | **KILL THE SEQUENCER MID-COMMIT: I1-I5 HOLD, THE SEAL SUCCEEDS, OFFSETS ARE NEVER REASSIGNED.** ⚠️ **I1-I5, NOT I1-I4**: architecture.md defines exactly five and M4's `VERIFIED.md` records I5 as the one a plausible implementation breaks. M4 asserted them across 1,000 SIMULATION seeds; this row asserts them across real process deaths, which is the difference between a model of the protocol and the protocol | FR-11, NFR-11 | done — `KillSequencerMidCommitIT`: three processes on MinIO with producers that fail over and retry. For three rounds the test reads the leader from the lease at the store, watches its chain, SIGKILLs it the moment a new delta lands, and starts a replacement. `ChainAudit` then reads every epoch's chain out of the bucket and checks I1 (each slot holds its own sequence, with no gap), I5's store half (no delta after its chain's first SEAL), the SEAL/CONTINUE link (each successor continues from exactly where its predecessor was sealed), and I2 over the effective history (each run starts where its stream's last one ended). The test then checks that every acked id is in a segment the effective history names, which is I4 and the ack half of I5. Measured: 3 kills, 3 SEALs, 3,300 acked, 0 lost, 0 violations. A successor continuing one slot short reds it, and so does one inheriting each offset one short (review). Duplicates vary by run, 0 and 100 have both been seen: a duplicate is a batch whose commit landed before the kill and whose ack never reached its producer |
| M8.12 | **`SIGSTOP` A SEQUENCER: THE GRAY-FAILURE ROW, WHICH RESEARCH 08 §9 SAYS FINDS REAL BUGS.** A stopped process renews no lease and answers no commit, so visibility must resume within a stated bound under a STRICTLY GREATER epoch -- and the stopped process, on `SIGCONT`, must commit NOTHING. ⚠️ **THAT LAST HALF IS EPOCH FENCING UNDER THE ONE FAILURE THAT PRODUCES A LIVE STALE LEADER**, which crash-stop testing cannot reach. ⚠️ **AND THE TEST ASSERTS LEASEHOLDER IDENTITY BEFORE STOPPING**, because `SIGSTOP` on a follower proves nothing and passes | FR-11, NFR-11 | done — `StoppedSequencerIT`: three processes on MinIO. The leader is identified from the lease, and its live term is asserted, before it is SIGSTOPped. Visibility (a delta under a new epoch, at the store) resumes in 4.4-4.8 s, against a bound of the TTL plus 5 s = 9 s. The new epoch is strictly greater. After SIGCONT, with producers still sending to it, the woken leader writes nothing past its old chain's SEAL. The audit is clean and nothing acked is lost: 8 of 8 consecutive runs on top of M8.49. Before M8.49, a leader's commit race produced an I2 violation in about 1 run in 8 (M8.48). ⚠️ **THE ROW FOUND A REAL BUG**: `IngesterNode.PEER_COMMIT_TIMEOUT` was a fixed 10 s, so every follower forwarded to the stopped leader for 10 s whatever the TTL, and visibility took 10.6 s. It is now the lease TTL. ⚠️ **AND TWO HARNESS FINDINGS**: a producer with no request timeout parked on the frozen node for the client's 30 s default, so the harness now times out at 2 s. And a delta PUT sent just before SIGSTOP can land while the process is frozen; it is sealed in, not a fencing failure. A writer that ignores a SEAL it loses a slot to reds the row |

Reassigned from M5, which ships membership as STATIC CONFIGURATION -- and a static list never removes a member, so the event a challenge fires on had no producer and NFR-9 would have read as met while real failover stayed TTL-bound at ~10 s. ⚠️ **ASSERTED AGAINST THE WATCH, NOT AGAINST THE TTL**: with the watch disabled the same test must take AT LEAST `leaseTtl` -- stated relative to the configured value because M8.27 is what MEASURES it, and an earlier draft of this row wrote `~10 s`, a constant this milestone owes measuring, or the number came from the timeout and the early-challenge path is not what produced it | NFR-9 | done — Four parts. `LeaseChallenge` is a seam in `LeaseManager`: an unexpired lease is taken only on evidence its holder is gone, and never from the pod itself. `EndpointSliceView` (pure, in http) turns watch events into that evidence: a holder is gone only if it was once seen ready and is now absent, or is neither ready nor terminating, because a draining pod is terminating and will release. `EndpointSliceWatch` streams the watch, sends the bearer token, and reconnects with backoff. Config keys `membership.kube-api/.namespace/.service/.token-file`; `Main` reads the token. `EarlyChallengeIT` uses a fake API server the test drives as the endpoints controller, with a 20 s TTL. With the watch, visibility resumed 335 ms after SIGKILL, and the new term committed while the killed leader's lease was still unexpired. Without it, 18.6 s (at least the TTL less one renew). ⚠️ **FOUND BY ITS OWN TEST**: closing Helidon's entity stream DRAINS it, so the first `close()` blocked for the 10-minute read timeout, which would have held a node's shutdown. It now interrupts the loop's virtual thread. ⚠️ HTTPS to a real API server needs the cluster CA, which is M8.51 |

A degraded path that costs per index is how a partition becomes a bill, and cost.md rule 6 is the rule it breaks. ⚠️ **THE ONE-INDEX FIXTURE EVERY OTHER TEST USES CANNOT SEE EITHER DEFECT.** -- which had no owning milestone in any roadmap row, backlog row or ADR until M5.21 gave it one. Pods partitioned from their sequencer but NOT from the store fall back to the commit-intent inbox (research 03 §10); when the partition heals, one epoch's writes are sealed and NO OFFSET IS ASSIGNED TWICE. ⚠️ **SAFETY HERE IS DETECTION-INDEPENDENT** (research 08 §4): dual leaders are expected and resolved by the seal, so the assertion is about DIVERGENCE, never about whether the partition was noticed | FR-11, NFR-3 | todo |

It also reports unhealthy after 3 consecutive failed WRITES, which is how a refusing store looks. Reads do not count, because a GET of a collected key fails legitimately. Review found the first draft kept a busy node unready over one hung connection, and counted reads. ⚠️ With the M8.13 watch on, an unready leader counts as gone and loses its term early. That is intended, because a leader that cannot reach the store cannot commit, and it is stated in both classes. `/ready` is now the drain gate AND store health; `/live` is untouched. `StorePartitionIT` black-holes one node's store proxy under 4 counting producers: 0 acks after a 3 s settle, unready 10.1 s after the cut, liveness still 200, and readiness plus acks back 209 ms after the heal. ⚠️ **FOUND BY ITS OWN TEST**: the `Assembly` constructor's parameter `store` shadowed the field, so the first wiring handed every consumer the UNTRACKED store and readiness never failed. The parameter is now `raw` |

A leader 5 min FAST, killed, still held the term 30 s later, past its 10 s TTL, because its expiry is its fast clock's, and nothing was acked meanwhile. | M8.33 | **`CommitRequestFrame` IN `format`, ITS ADR AND ITS GOLDEN FILES** (split out of M8.20, which needs bytes on a wire before it can have a socket). ⚠️ **A WIRE-FORMAT CHANGE**, so it carries the `wire-format-change` obligations: the format, every reader, every writer, the fakes, the goldens and the ADR in ONE commit. **ADR-0053**: a forwarded commit is a format type, not a JSON body -- `incarnationId` exists because `(podId, flushSeq)` cannot tell a RESTART from a REPLAY (ADR-0036), and a loose encoding is one where that field is the easiest to drop as noise, on the path where the answer decides whether records are committed twice or not at all. ⚠️ **AND THIS IS THE ONE FORMAT WHERE A ROLLING DEPLOY GUARANTEES MIXED VERSIONS**: unlike a segment, which one build writes and another reads later, a forwarded commit is sent by the pod being replaced TO the pod replacing it, at exactly the moment their builds differ. ⚠️ **DONE.** `CommitRequestFrame` (magic `BPCR`, v1) with 23 cases and two golden files -- the two-stream fixture and the SMALLEST LEGAL REQUEST, because a zero `flushSeq` and a single-entry count are where an off-by-one in the varints shows and the larger fixture hides it. ⚠️ **AND THE GOLDEN FIXTURE's STREAMS ARE INSERTED IN THE ORDER THE SORT MUST UNDO**: with them inserted already-sorted, an encoder that DROPPED the sort produced the same bytes and the golden stayed GREEN -- measured, and the fixture only discriminates because its insertion order is the wrong one. The refusals are the usual set and each is about this path: an unknown version STOPS rather than skipping, `!=` rather than `>` because a version BELOW the known one is what zeroed torn bytes carry, the stream count is bounded by the bytes remaining BEFORE it becomes an allocation (a torn frame claiming 0x7FFFFFFF streams kills the loop serving the peer past its own `throws IOException`, which stops the fleet committing), trailing bytes are a refusal, a duplicate stream is undecidable in both directions, and a zero record count is REFUSED rather than dropped -- a stream named with no records is a caller that believes it wrote something. ⚠️ **AND REVIEW MEASURED THE ONE THAT WAS MISSING**: with `toInt`'s bound removed, a varint partition of `0x80000000` NARROWED to a negative int and `RunKey`'s own guard threw an `IllegalArgumentException` straight out of `decode` -- past its `throws IOException`, which is the unchecked throw that kills the loop serving the peer and stops the fleet committing -- and the whole suite stayed green. The construction is now inside the boundary and both narrowing reads have a case. ⚠️ **A STREAM ID IS TWO BIG-ENDIAN LONGS AND NOT A STRING**, which is how every other `RunKey` in the package goes on a wire: 16 bytes rather than 37, and `UUID.fromString` is lenient enough that `0-0-4000-8000-1` and the canonical form are two byte sequences decoding to one id, which contradicts the determinism this format promises. ⚠️ **AND THE FRAME ENFORCES EVERY GUARD `CommitRequest` DOES, INCLUDING THE podId GRAMMAR** (round 2, and the same class of defect as the narrowing one): a peer's `pod-1` decoded cleanly and then threw an unchecked `IllegalArgumentException` out of the conversion, in the loop serving that peer -- and the grammar is not cosmetic, since a segment key is `<seq>-<podId>-<flushSeq>-...` | FR-11, FR-12 | done |

The retention loop calls it through the fenced store after the segment pass (`RetentionLoop.Term.chainGc`), which an idle tick never reaches. `ChainGc` now judges "below the checkpoint" in (epoch, sequence): by sequence alone it kept a predecessor's deltas for ever and would collect a newer epoch's. NOT collected, and staying in the bucket: a predecessor's checkpoints, which this term never wrote and cannot know without a LIST; a predecessor's deltas whose segments were still kept at the takeover (the delta twin of M8.42(a)); deltas dropped once the awaiting list passes `maxDeltas`, and checkpoints past `CheckpointWriter.MAX_WRITTEN`; and whatever was awaiting only in memory when the process restarted |

| M8.42 | **WHAT THE RETENTION LOOP CANNOT REACH AFTER A TAKEOVER, NAMED AS STORAGE RATHER THAN SOLVED** (opened by M8.5). A replay stops at a checkpoint, so a new term's in-memory chain lacks every delta below the newest one. Two consequences, both in the safe direction and both unbounded over enough takeovers: (a) a segment named ONLY by a pre-checkpoint delta is never judged by the new term's pass, so it is never collected; (b) the orphan sweep is confined to hours starting at least an hour after the term was first seen (`RetentionLoop.SKEW_MARGIN`), so orphans in the hours before that are never swept by that term. ⚠️ **THE CONFINEMENT IN (b) IS NOT OPTIONAL AND MUST NOT BE "FIXED" BY WIDENING IT**: without it the sweep's keep list is missing the pre-checkpoint deltas, and it FAILS OPEN -- a committed segment is listed, found in no delta, and deleted. The fix for both is a keep list that is complete back to the retention floor, which is what a checkpoint deliberately does not carry (it is not the offset-to-segment index) | FR-9, NFR-3 | todo |

The liveness cost of a fast pod is therefore the dead-fast-leader case (a takeover delayed by the skew), which the second case already pins. Red: a lease comparison that never finds one expired |

| M8.20 | **THE PRODUCTION `SequencerTransport`** (M5.6e, owed to M8 since M5 and absent from this spec's first decomposition -- found by its review). `InProcessTransport` in `testFixtures` is the ONLY implementation in the tree: M5.3 scoped "the seam and its fake" and nothing ever owned the real one, so `new FleetSequencer(store, config, ???, election)` cannot be written by a service today. ⚠️ **WITHOUT IT THERE IS NO FLEET TO BREAK**: every append to a non-leaseholder pod fails, and M8.9-M8.17 would be chaos rows over a single pod talking to itself. ⚠️ **DONE.** `HttpSequencerTransport` (the client) and `CommitService` (the route) in `http`, with 21 cases over a REAL socket. ⚠️ **THE THREE OUTCOMES ARE THE SUBJECT, NOT THE HAPPY PATH**: 200 carries the delta; **409** is a REFUSAL -- nothing was applied, so re-reading the lease and resending is safe -- and everything else, including a connect failure and a timeout, is an `IOException` whose message says the outcome is UNKNOWN. ⚠️ **A TIMEOUT MUST NEVER BECOME A 409**: that one conversion turns an ambiguous outcome into a licence to resend elsewhere, which is how one flush gets two ranges of offsets, and it is what every plausible-wrong stub of this class does first. ⚠️ **A FENCED SEQUENCER ANSWERS 409 TOO**, because it means the same thing to the caller; mapping it to 500 would make a clean refusal ambiguous and stall every write on the forwarding node. ⚠️ **A MALFORMED FRAME IS 400 AND NOT 409**: a frame this node cannot read says nothing about who holds the lease, and 409 would send the peer hunting for a leaseholder over a version skew, for ever. ⚠️ **THE SEQUENCER IS SUPPLIED LAZILY** -- leadership moves, and a captured reference commits through a sequencer whose lease is gone, which epoch fencing catches one layer too late, after the node has answered 200. ⚠️ **AND REVIEW's OWN METHOD FOUND THREE SURVIVING MUTATIONS BEFORE THE REVIEWER DID**: a captured sequencer (the case only moved null -> live, never back), a closed transport (the case sent to a DEAD address, so it failed for the other reason), and the ambiguous-to-refusal conversion in one of TWO catch arms, absorbed by the sibling -- the arms are now one method, so there is one place to get it wrong. ⚠️ **AND REVIEW FOUND FOUR MORE**, two of them security- and liveness-shaped: the route read the WHOLE entity before decoding, so M8.33's stream-count bound -- bought so a torn frame cannot become an allocation -- applied only AFTER the allocation it exists to prevent, and a `Content-Length: 8000000000` POST would OOM the leaseholder every other node forwards to (security.md rule 5); the client pool had no bound, and since the endpoint comes from the LEASE and a rescheduled pod returns at a NEW address, it grew with peer INCARNATIONS for the process's life; the two-hop refusal arm was executed by NO case, so a chained clean refusal could have been mapped to an ambiguous 500 with the suite green; and the configured timeout was pinned by nothing, because the dead-address case is connection-REFUSED and never touches a clock. ⚠️ **AND ROUND 2 FOUND THE SHARPEST ONE**: Helidon's `Status.equals` COMPARES THE REASON PHRASE as well as the code -- `Status.create(409, "").equals(Status.CONFLICT_409)` is FALSE, and RFC 9112 permits an empty reason -- so a peer answering 409 with a non-canonical reason had its CLEAN REFUSAL read as ambiguous, and a 200 with an odd reason reported a commit that WAS APPLIED as UNKNOWN. Invisible to every case whose peer is this repository's own `CommitService`, which is all of them. Compared by `.code()` now, with a case on each side. ⚠️ **AND A 413 IS A KNOWN OUTCOME, NOT AN UNKNOWN ONE**: nothing was decoded, so nothing was applied -- but resending it unchanged will be refused again, so it is neither ambiguous nor a leaseholder problem. ⚠️ **AND ROUND 3 FOUND ROUND 1's FINDING IN THE MIRROR**: the PEER's REPLY was buffered whole with no bound, and a lazily generated 2 GiB 200 response killed the CLIENT with an `OutOfMemoryError` -- an Error escaping `send` past its own `throws IOException`, taking the forwarding node's writer with it. The peer need not be hostile; one running a build with a different idea of a delta is enough. ⚠️ **AND `MAX_POOLED_CLIENTS = 1` LEFT ALL NINETEEN CASES GREEN**, because the reuse case used ONE endpoint and the bound case read the constant -- so the pool's size meant nothing until a case with TWO live peers existed | FR-11, FR-12 | done |
| M8.21 | **THE PRODUCTION `SubscriptionTransport`, AND `IndexRegistrar.onReconnect()` WIRED TO ITS RECONNECT** (M6.15, which names the missing transport as its blocker). ⚠️ **CRITERION 1's CONSUMER HAS NOTHING TO SUBSCRIBE OVER WITHOUT IT**, and M6.19's routed path reaches a deployable server only through it. ⚠️ **DONE.** `SubscriptionService` (the ingester's half) and `HttpSubscriptionTransport` (the consumer's), with 20 cases over a real socket: a push out, a registration back, a progress frame back -- ONE channel, three messages, which is the design rather than a shortcut, because a second endpoint is a second credential and a second unauthenticated surface. ⚠️ **AND `IndexRegistrar.onReconnect()` FINALLY HAS A CALLER** (M6.15) -- `NodeChannel`, which exists because the transport and the registrar refer to each other and neither can be constructed with the other in hand, so "the caller wires it" left it wired nowhere for two milestones: a registration is state the INGESTER holds in memory, so a restarted ingester knows no index's shape and every routed write to this node is refused when its wait expires, with nothing naming the cause. ⚠️ **A LONG POLL, NOT AN OPEN STREAM, AND THAT WAS MEASURED RATHER THAN PREFERRED**: the first implementation held a chunked response open and wrote length-prefixed frames into it, and Helidon's client stream does not block across a chunk boundary -- every heartbeat arrived and the first real frame killed the connection with `InsufficientDataAvailableException`, byte-at-a-time reads included. ⚠️ **AND THE SUBSCRIPTION MUST OUTLIVE THE POLL**: subscribing to the hub for the duration of ONE poll meant a push published in the GAP between two polls reached nobody -- no gap, no error, just records the consumer would never be delivered, and since a consumer's position is its own (ADR-0005) nothing downstream would have noticed. Sessions are keyed by a client-supplied id, swept when idle, and the sweep runs on another consumer's poll rather than on a timer. ⚠️ **THE COST IS AN HTTP REQUEST PER SUBSCRIPTION PER 30 s WHEN IDLE** -- a same-AZ peer request, never an object-store one, so NFR-2 is untouched -- and a burst costs ONE round trip rather than one poll per push, because an answer drains everything queued. ⚠️ **THREE BOUNDS, EACH MEASURED RATHER THAN ASSUMED**: the poll answer is capped WHILE it is read rather than after (a cap applied after `readAllBytes` never runs -- the allocation fails first, with an `OutOfMemoryError` that is an `Error` and escapes the poll loop's catch, killing the subscription's thread in silence inside the OpenSearch node process); the session map is capped at 4,096, because the `sub` id is the CALLER'S on a route this deployment does not authenticate and a fresh id per poll allocates a hub subscription and a 64-push queue faster than any sweep can reclaim them; and the poll wait is clamped by the ingester, since `?wait=0` is a busy loop against it and `?wait=3600000` is an hour of its thread | FR-16, FR-13 | done |
| M8.22 | **THE S3 BACKEND THROUGH THE STORE CONFORMANCE SUITE**, including the branches only a real backend reaches. Split from M8.2 by this spec's review: the backend's verbs and the suite that judges them are two commits, and the suite is where MinIO-versus-S3 divergence surfaces. ⚠️ **DONE, AND IT FOUND SIX THINGS THE BACKEND's OWN 17 CASES DID NOT.** Three were defects in the backend, each now fixed: an inverted range threw `IllegalArgumentException` where the contract and every other backend answer `IOException`; a conditional write ranked a BAD BODY above a MOVED VERSION, where the contract says a stale version yields empty even when the body is bad (M2.0's review found the in-memory backend making the same mistake); and the advertised key limit was S3's 1,024 where the endpoint in front of it refuses at 255 -- a capability is a promise checked at STARTUP, so it describes the endpoint, which is why it is configuration now. ⚠️ **THREE ARE THE FIXTURE's AND ARE DISABLED WITH THE MEASUREMENT RATHER THAN WEAKENED IN THE SUITE**: MinIO's listing is not a byte range (measured against the RAW SDK with none of this project's code involved -- `p/a`, `p/ab`, `p/a/x` written, HEAD finds all three, `ListObjectsV2` with prefix `p/a` answers two), and its conditional writes drop connections under 8-way contention with both HTTP clients and with SDK retries on and off, which is testing.md rule 19a measured again. ⚠️ **AND THE SDK NO LONGER RETRIES**: a retried `putIfAbsent` whose first attempt SUCCEEDED answers 412 on the retry, which this backend would report as "someone else got there" -- a writer that won being told it lost, in the one place the commit log cannot tell the difference | NFR-8 | done |
| M8.23 | **THE HARNESS's NETWORK HALF: PARTITION A PROCESS FROM A PEER AND FROM THE STORE, AND SKEW ITS CLOCK.** Split from M8.8, which owns start/kill/stop. ⚠️ **THE SKEW IS APPLIED TO THE PROCESS, NOT THROUGH THE `Clock` SEAM** -- every pod takes a `Clock`, so injecting a skewed one tests the seam and leaves a lease comparison reading the wall clock directly untouched, which is exactly the mutation M8.17 exists to catch | FR-11 | done — `ChaosProxy` is a TCP forwarder the test can cut. A cut is a BLACK HOLE, not a refusal, and a heal loses nothing: while cut the proxy stops READING both ways, so buffers fill and senders block, and the heal resumes each stream intact, as TCP does over a real partition. A connection made while cut is held and joined at the heal. ⚠️ Review found the first draft discarding bytes while cut, which handed a healed receiver a torn stream; `ChaosProxyTest` pins the fix. In front of the store it cuts one node from the bucket; in front of a node's advertised endpoint (`NodeProcess.Options.peerProxy`) it isolates the node from its peers, inbound only. `SkewAgent` is a `-javaagent` that rewrites every non-JDK class as it loads, redirecting `System.currentTimeMillis`, `Instant.now`, `Clock.systemUTC`/`systemDefaultZone` and the date-time `now()`s, so the skew covers this project's code and every library, the AWS SDK's signing included. The JDK's own reads and monotonic time are untouched. `ChaosNetworkIT`: a store cut stops the acks, and the bucket sees nothing until the heal. A peer cut fails the follower's forwarded write while the leader still writes. A +5 min process writes a lease expiring 308,967 ms from true now, and `SkewProbe` shows its DIRECT reads (`currentTimeMillis`, `Instant.now`, `Clock.systemUTC`) are 5 min ahead too. Review found the lease check alone passing with the direct redirects deleted, because it goes through the seam. The agent skips bootstrap and platform classes, and its javadoc lists exactly what it does not move. Each assertion reds when its verb is a no-op |

non-positive duration (`Duration.parse` accepts `PT-5S`, which is a renewer that never sleeps), a negative or zero size, a size with a unit suffix, ⚠️ **AN UNKNOWN KEY** -- the typo case, and the one most likely to be argued about: a tolerated `lease.tll` leaves the TTL at its default, the node runs, and the operator's evidence that they changed it is their own config file -- and ⚠️ **A MISSPELLED BOOLEAN**, because `Boolean.parseBoolean` reads everything but `true` as FALSE and this flag's true value is what makes a node refuse to start against a backend that cannot sign (M5.43), so a typo silently disables the check it was set to turn on. ⚠️ **IT TAKES A MAP AND READS NO FILE**: reading one would name `java.nio.file` in the one module that is NOT exempt from `check-io-seam.sh`, and where the map comes from is the process's business (M8.4). ⚠️ **AND `store.root` WAS THE ONE READ THAT SKIPPED THE BLANK CHECK** (found by review, MEASURED): `store.root=${DATA_DIR}` with DATA_DIR unset parsed to a root of three spaces and `StoreFactory` RETURNED A STORE rooted at a directory named `   ` under the process's working directory -- a node that starts, acks, and writes where nothing reads, which is this row's opening sentence happening in the one setting allowed to skip its own rule. ⚠️ **AND EVERY CONSTRUCTION IS NOW INSIDE THE ONE WRAPPER**: `ingest.interval-floor=PT10S` is positive, well-formed and passes every check in the parser, and was refused by `IngestConfig` as a bare `IllegalArgumentException` naming no key the operator wrote -- cross-field rules live in the records, because a second copy of them here goes stale. ⚠️ **THE EXIT CODE HALF OF CRITERION 21 IS M8.4's** -- a T0 parser test passes against a root that catches the refusal and starts degraded | The alternative, banning every `now(`, refuses `LocalDate.now(clock)`, which review has already flagged once as the false refusal most likely to get a gate edited rather than obeyed ⚠️ **AND A STATIC FACTORY IS CALLED ON A TYPE**: the `.now()` and `.now(...)` entries anchor their receiver to a capitalised name, so a record accessor named `now()` reaches nothing and passes. ⚠️ THE BARE-CALL ENTRIES `currentTimeMillis(` AND `nanoTime(` ARE DELIBERATELY UNANCHORED, because a static import leaves no receiver at all -- so a record component named `nanoTime` IS refused, which review measured and which M0.112 records -- review measured the unanchored form refusing a file that touched no clock |

| M0.109 | `check-backlog-table.sh` -- every row in `backlog.md` has the same cell count as its table's header. ⚠️ **OPENED BY M5.10 ROUND-4 REVIEW**, which caught the M5.10 row itself carrying FIVE cells in a four-column table. GFM DISCARDS cells past the header count, so the rendered State column showed the acceptance criteria and the word `done` did not appear in the row at all -- exactly the defect `e9e56aa` (M0.105) had just finished repairing by hand across every M5 row, reintroduced one commit later. ⚠️ TWO INSTANCES REMAIN IN THE TREE and M5.10 does NOT fix them, deliberately, because they belong to other rows: `M5.34` (landed with M5.27) and `M0.26`, both at seven pipes against a five-pipe header, both because a code span in the prose contains a literal `\|` -- `!hasPointer() \|\| incarnationId() == null` in the first -- which GFM splits on even inside backticks. ⚠️ THE RULE IS A PREDICATE OVER FILES IN THE TREE, so non-negotiable 9 puts it at rung 3 and forbids asking an agent for it: count unescaped pipes per row, compare against the header of the table that row is in, and name the row ID. The fix it should suggest is `\|` inside the span, which GFM honours in a table cell even inside a code span. ⚠️ AND IT MUST BE PER TABLE -- `backlog.md` holds six tables and the last has three columns, so one global count would fail every row in it ⚠️ **TWO INSTANCES FOUND AT M5.32, ONE FIXED THERE AND ONE STILL OPEN.** `M5.34`'s row carried two UNESCAPED pipes inside `` `!hasPointer() \|\| incarnationId() == null` ``, giving it SIX raw cells against a four-column header -- GFM dropped its `FR-11` and its `todo`, so the row rendered with no Serves and no State at all. M5.32 escaped them, because it was rewriting that row anyway. **`M0.26` is still at six** and is nobody's to fix in passing. ⚠️ AND M5.32 BRIEFLY ADDED A THIRD, in this very row, by writing `\|\|` unescaped while describing the defect -- which is the argument for the gate rather than for more care. That is worse than the M5.10 case this row was opened for, because a dropped `todo` reads as a row with no state rather than a row with an extra one. ⚠️ TWO RULES, AND THEY ARE NOT THE SAME ONE -- an earlier draft of this sentence collapsed them into "count cells after accounting for code spans", which reads as IGNORE PIPES INSIDE CODE SPANS and would make the gate compute four cells for the pre-fix `M5.34` row and PASS it. Not the instance that OPENED this row -- M5.10's extra cell is structural and fails either way -- but the one M5.32 found, which is the whole reason the rule needs stating. (a) A BACKSLASH-ESCAPED pipe never splits a cell, inside a code span or out. (b) A BARE pipe DOES split, **even inside backticks** -- that is the defect, not a false positive to suppress. So the gate counts BARE pipes and cares nothing about code spans. ⚠️ AND M5.32 INTRODUCED THIS DEFECT INTO THIS ROW **TWICE** while describing it, once with `\|\|` and once with a bare pipe. That is the argument for the gate: three instances in one commit, two of them written by someone who had the rule in front of them ⚠️ **AND A THIRD SHAPE, FOUND AT M0.107: A NEWLINE INSIDE A CELL.** Editing the M0.107 State cell with text carrying an embedded newline split ONE row into THREE lines -- the tail became two orphan lines that render as body text outside the table, and the appended paragraph landed TWICE because the same edit was applied twice to a file that already held it. The row was then left with five raw cells and no trailing pipe. ⚠️ SO THE GATE NEEDS A SECOND HALF: besides counting unescaped pipes per row, refuse a line that sits inside a table and does not begin with a pipe. Neither the cell count nor the pipe rule sees this one -- the orphan lines are not rows at all. ⚠️ AND THE DEFECT THIS ROW IS NAMED FOR WAS INTRODUCED INTO THIS ROW A THIRD TIME while writing this very sentence: a bare \| inside a code span, caught by an assertion in the editing helper rather than by a gate. Three authors' worth of care have now failed on the same line, which is the argument for rung 3. | — | todo |

M5.42 needs the same backend for its security.md rule 4 case and is reassigned in ITS OWN commit, immediately after this one -- not folded in here, so the reassignment of each row is traceable to a commit that names it. ⚠️ UNTIL THAT LANDS THE TREE IS INCONSISTENT: the roadmap books M5.42's case into M9 while M5.42's own State cell still reads `todo`, and review named that window. ⚠️ AND (2) IS NOT REASSIGNED, BECAUSE IT IS NOT A SCHEDULING PROBLEM: a backend doing a `stat` or an `iam.signBlob` round trip INSIDE its own `presign` is invisible to any meter wrapped AROUND it -- measured, a stand-in doing `stat(key)` before signing survived both this suite and a `CountingBinStore` -- so no test above the backend can ever buy it, and a later milestone would inherit the same impossibility. It rests on `BinStore.presign`'s javadoc, which is rung 7, and non-negotiable 9 asks why 1-5 cannot carry it: rung 1 would need the round trip unrepresentable inside code this project does not own, and rung 3 would need a scanner over `presign` implementations, and every one in the tree is a TEST STAND-IN. ⚠️ REVIEW CAUGHT THIS CELL SAYING "ZERO", WHICH IS FALSE AND WHICH THE CELL ITSELF REFUTES TWO SENTENCES EARLIER: `CountingBinStoreTest.CapableStore`, `GrantIssuerTest`'s and `GrantNeverLoggedTest.SigningStore` all MINT a `SignedUrl`, and a dozen more decorators in `sequencer` forward one. M5.42 is the second, so it reads `todo, owned by **M8** -- ADR-0052 moved the first real backend there, and this case is one of the two things that land with it (M8.18)`. ⚠️ AND THIS CORRECTION'S OWN FIRST DRAFT CLAIMED THE TABLE "HAS TWO DISTINCT STATE IDIOMS", WHICH REVIEW MEASURED FALSE: `split -- see ...` is a third, at M5.15, M5.40 and M5.45, with one-offs besides. | M5.38 | THE TWO INLINE BOUNDS ARE INDEPENDENT AND THE SMALLER ONE IS NOT A CEILING. `FetchPolicyConfig` derives `crossAzCrossoverBytes` from the price table and takes `inlineCapBytes` as a dial, and nothing relates them. ⚠️ ROUND-2 REVIEW MEASURED THE INCOHERENCE: at `crossAzMicroDollarsPerGb = 1_000` the derived crossover is 400,000 bytes against a 262,144-byte cap, so a 300 KiB batch is INLINED when its bytes must cross an AZ and answered `PROXY` when they are local and FREE -- the cheaper path taking the more expensive mode. ⚠️ THE CAP IS DOCUMENTED AS PROTECTING THE PUSH CHANNEL AND THE CONSUMER'S HEAP, and neither of those cares which AZ the bytes came from, so it should bound BOTH paths: the fix is one `Math.min` in `modeFor`, or a constructor invariant. ⚠️ NOT FIXED IN M5.11 BECAUSE IT IS PRODUCTION LOGIC AND M5.11's THIRD ROUND WAS SIGNED UNDER THE STANDING M5 AUTHORITY, whose third condition is that the round changes NO production logic. Taking it would have voided the signature; deferring it keeps the condition true and the change visible. ⚠️ IT IS ALSO UNREACHABLE TODAY: `defaultsFor` is the only production caller, and against an S3-priced table its crossover is 20,000 bytes -- an order of magnitude inside the cap. ⚠️ Round-3 review corrected this in the direction of SAFETY: both shipping backends report `CostTable.free()`, so today's derived crossover is actually 0, further inside the cap than the S3 figure suggests | FR-6 | ⚠️ **done** -- one `Math.min` in `modeFor`: the inline cap bounds BOTH paths and the cross-AZ crossover bounds the billed one further. The cap protects the push channel and the consumer's heap, and neither cares which AZ the bytes came from, so applying it to the same-AZ path alone was what let the order invert. ⚠️ TWO CASES, and the ordering is the property rather than the single point: `aCROSSAZBatchABOVETheInlineCapIsNotINLINED` is this row's measured example (a 400,000-byte crossover against a 262,144 cap inlining 300 KiB cross-AZ), and `theCROSSAZPathNeverInlinesASizeTheSAMEAZPathWouldNot` asserts the relation over eight sizes spanning both bounds IN ONE HARDCODED CONFIG. ⚠️ IT IS NOT A PROPERTY OVER CONFIGS and an earlier draft of this cell said it was: the case never reads the config back, so at a 1 MiB cap against a 2 MiB crossover every sampled size inlines on both paths and it stays green. What makes the relation hold for ALL inputs is the code, not the case -- `INLINE` depends only on `batchBytes <= inlineBound`, and the billed bound is now `min(cap, crossover)`, which is at most the free one. Both were observed failing first: the point case at 300,000 bytes, the ordering case at 262,145. ⚠️ THE POINT CASE ALSO PINS THE BOUND FROM BELOW, which the first round did not: review MEASURED `min(inlineCapBytes() / 2, ...)` and `inlineCapBytes() - 1` surviving the WHOLE module, because both cases were one-sided -- a strictly tighter cross-AZ bound satisfies "not INLINE above the cap" and the ordering relation trivially. An assertion that a batch exactly AT the cap still inlines kills both. ⚠️ THE CONSTRUCTOR INVARIANT WAS THE OTHER OPTION THIS ROW OFFERED AND IS NOT WHAT LANDED, deliberately: rejecting a config whose crossover exceeds the cap would make `derivedFrom` throw at any transfer price below about 1,500 micro-dollars/GB, and a low price is legitimate rather than a misconfiguration. Bounding at the decision keeps the derivation total. ⚠️ STILL UNREACHABLE IN PRODUCTION, as this row recorded: `defaultsFor` is the only production caller and both shipping backends report `CostTable.free()`, so today's derived crossover is 0 and already inside the cap. The fix is for the price table that has not shipped yet |
| M5.39 | WHOEVER WIRES `FetchPolicy` OWES A COALESCING RULE. ⚠️ **M5.40 IS THE SAME RULE ON THE `proxy` PATH** -- one serve per (node, segment) rather than per run -- and the two should be taken together. ⚠️ ROUND-3 REVIEW OF M5.11: a `SegmentDelivery` is ONE BATCH EVENT -- its own `batchBytes` says "this stream's share of the commit, not the whole segment" -- so at `segmentFanOut == 1` with cold bytes, ONE catch-up node holding K shards' runs inside a segment is answered `DIRECT` K times and issues K GETs. Doc 10 §4's model behind the rule prices `direct cost = N GETs` with N = CONSUMERS, so the cold conjunct M5.11 added restores flatness in NODE count and not in shards-per-node. ⚠️ THE CORPUS CONTEMPLATES THE GAP BUT NOTHING DISCHARGES IT: doc 10 §7 gives the client "read coalescing -- merge adjacent ranges before requesting, in every mode", and §9 records grants as per-object -- but one node's runs inside a ~1,600-run segment are generally NOT adjacent, so adjacent-range merging does not collapse K to 1. ⚠️ IT IS NOT A DEFECT IN `modeFor`, which is why M5.11 did not change it: the policy answers per event and answers correctly per event. The obligation is on the CALLER -- one grant per (node, segment) rather than per run -- and it belongs in M5.13's `direct` wiring, in M5.16's prefetch, or in M5's SPEC as a criterion. Whoever takes it states which. ⚠️ **M5.13 STATED IT**: `GrantIssuer.grantFor`'s javadoc now carries "one grant per (node, segment), never one per run", and records that each grant is one consumer-side GET INVISIBLE to the ingester's own `CountingBinStore` -- so nothing in this process can measure issuing too many. The rule is written; ENFORCING it is M5.43's, with the wiring | FR-6 | ⚠️ **done -- DISCHARGED BY THE ROWS THAT DID THE WORK, and this closes the bookkeeping rather than adding code.** This row's deliverable was a STATEMENT, not an enforcement: "the obligation is on the CALLER ... Whoever takes it states which". Both halves are stated and both citations were checked against the tree rather than against this row's own prose. ⚠️ **THE `direct` HALF**, `GrantIssuer.java:140`: "ONE GRANT PER (NODE, SEGMENT), NEVER ONE PER RUN", landed by M5.13, which also records that each grant is one consumer-side GET INVISIBLE to the ingester's `CountingBinStore` -- so nothing in this process can measure issuing too many -- and that the API does not resist it, minting fresh every call. ⚠️ **THE `proxy` HALF**, `SegmentProxy.java:130`: "Call it ONCE PER SEGMENT with every interested sink, not once per run", landed by M5.12, with the shard-scaling figure this row derived (~1,600 GETs for one 8 MiB segment if a caller loops the per-`RunKey` map) and the second granularity beside it -- one entry per CONSUMER, not one per subscription, or a node holding ~178 runs receives the same 8 MiB 178 times while the GET count stays 1 and every test stays green. ⚠️ **ENFORCEMENT IS TRACKED AND NOT DONE, WHICH IS THE HONEST STATE**: M5.43 owns the `direct` wiring that must respect the grant rule, M5.40 owns the `proxy` half, and both are open. Neither statement is a gate -- each sits at rung 7 by gate-design's ladder, and `SegmentProxy`'s own javadoc says no test can see it because every test in that file measures a single call. ⚠️ CLOSING THIS ROW ASSERTS ONLY THAT THE RULE IS WRITTEN WHERE A CALLER READS IT, which is what it asked for |
| M5.40 | `SegmentProxy` DELIVERS ONE READ PER CALL, NOT ONE PER NODE, so cost.md R5 is not yet met for `proxy`. ⚠️ ROUND-1 REVIEW OF M5.12 caught the javadoc claiming R5 -- "one fetch per object per NODE, shared by every shard on it" -- where the code has no cache and coalesces nothing in flight, so every call is a fresh GET. ⚠️ THE TRIGGER IS IN M5's SPEC's OWN FETCH-MODE TABLE: it names "a late subscriber" as a reason to serve `proxy`, and a late subscriber is BY CONSTRUCTION not in a list already served -- so one fan-out of 64 plus three late subscribers is four GETs for one object on one node. Doc 10 §4's "AZ recovery: many nodes replaying the same backlog" is the same shape at scale. ⚠️ EVERY M5.12 TEST PASSES REGARDLESS, because all of them measure a single call -- the gap is invisible to that whole file. ⚠️ WHAT CLOSES IT: SPEC rule R11 puts the cache in the ingester pods and M5.16 owns the per-AZ prefetch; `BinStore` is `SegmentProxy`'s only byte source today, so serving a cached segment means changing that. Whoever takes it decides whether the cache sits behind the store seam or beside it. ⚠️ AND THE PERMITTED HALF IS ONLY HALF: round-2 review pointed out this row records per-NODE repetition, while the same absence of a call-granularity contract permits something the requirements actually forbid -- a caller looping the per-`RunKey` subscriber map issues one GET per SHARD per flush, ~1,600 for one 8 MiB segment, which IS the shard scaling NFR-4 rules out. `streamTo`'s javadoc now says "once per segment, not once per run", but that is prose at rung 7 and no test can see it: every test in `SegmentProxyTest` measures a single call. ⚠️ AND THE TWO HALVES CLOSE DIFFERENTLY, which round-3 review pointed out this row conflated: the CACHE closes the per-node half (SPEC R11, M5.16's prefetch), and it does NOT close the caller-granularity half -- a caller looping runs still calls once per run, and a cache merely makes the repeats cheap rather than absent. What closes THAT half is a call-site rule, and it is the same rule **M5.39** states for `direct`: one grant, or one serve, per (node, segment) rather than per run. ⚠️ THE TWO ROWS ARE ONE PROBLEM ON TWO PATHS and neither named the other until now; whoever takes either should take both, or say why the answer differs | NFR-4 | ⚠️ **split -- see M5.40a and M5.40b**, and the CALLER-GRANULARITY half this row opened with is already CLOSED, which no round of this row noticed. ⚠️ **CLOSED BY M5.45a, AND A TEST DOES SEE IT**: `SubscriptionHub.publishSegment` gathers targets across every run of a segment and calls `streamTo` ONCE for the segment, and `AssembledServingPathTest.aSegmentOfMANYRunsIsReadONCENotOncePerRun` counts the reads through `CountingBinStore`. ⚠️ SO THIS ROW'S OWN "no test can see it: every test in `SegmentProxyTest` measures a single call" WAS TRUE OF THAT FILE AND STALE ABOUT THE TREE -- the test that sees it lives at the CALL SITE, which is the only place that can see it, and looking only where the prose lived is what kept the row open. The ~1,600-GETs-per-flush shape NFR-4 forbids is therefore not reachable through this caller. ⚠️ **WHAT REMAINS IS TWO PROBLEMS THAT CLOSE DIFFERENTLY**, which round-3 review said this row conflated and which is why it is split rather than closed: **M5.40a** is one byte stream per CONSUMER within one publish, and **M5.40b** is one fetch per NODE across SEPARATE publishes. A cache answers the second and not the first; a call-site rule answers the first and not the second |
| M5.40a | **ONE BYTE STREAM PER CONSUMER, NOT ONE PER SUBSCRIPTION.** `SubscriptionHub.publishSegment` builds one `Target` per (subscription, run), so a node subscribed to several runs of the same segment is opened several times and handed the same bytes once per run it hosts -- around 178 of a segment's ~1,600 runs for an 8 MiB segment. ⚠️ **IT IS NFR-5, NOT NFR-4, AND AN EARLIER DRAFT OF THIS ROW FILED IT WRONG.** The GET count stays 1 -- `aSegmentOfMANYRunsIsReadONCENotOncePerRun` passes -- so the READ RATE that NFR-4 governs is not at risk, and a taker reading NFR-4 would write an acceptance criterion against a number that is already correct. What breaks is NFR-5, cross-AZ bytes under 0.1% of ingested bytes: one `Target` per (subscription, run) sends 1,600 runs x 8 MiB = 12,800 MiB = **12.5 GiB** of pushes for ONE 8 MiB segment, 1,600x ingested bytes with roughly two thirds of it crossing an AZ. Against a 0.1% budget that is 1,600 / 0.001 = **six** orders of magnitude over, and still six charging only the cross-AZ share. ⚠️ **BUT THE ACCEPTANCE CRITERION IS PROPORTIONALITY, NOT THE 0.1% ITSELF**, and getting that wrong is the mirror of the NFR-4 mistake above: the fix CANNOT REACH 0.1% either. One stream per consumer still sends 8 MiB to each of ~9 nodes, ~6x ingested bytes cross-AZ, three orders over the target -- reaching it needs the per-AZ prefetch M5.16 owns, not this row. What this row can be held to, and what a taker should assert, is that pushed bytes scale with CONSUMERS per segment and never with RUNS per segment, which is why "waste" was the wrong word for it. `SegmentProxy`'s javadoc states the rule ("ONE ENTRY PER CONSUMER, not one per subscription") and names this row as carrying it. ⚠️ IT IS A CONTRACT CHANGE, NOT AN EDIT, and that is why M5.40 was split rather than fixed: each `Push` carries its OWN run's key, record count and first offset, so a consumer subscribed to K runs legitimately needs K PUSHES while needing the bytes ONCE. Whoever takes it decides how `Subscriber` expresses that -- one `open` per segment returning one sink with K pushes attached, or an `open` that takes the whole list -- and says which. ⚠️ THE FAILURE PATHS FOLLOW THE SINK, so merging sinks merges outcomes: today a throw for one run drops that run's subscriber and leaves the others, and `Tracking` completes only sinks that took every byte. With one sink per consumer a mid-stream throw must fail ALL K of that consumer's runs, or none, and the row must say which | NFR-5 | ⚠️ **done in `ingest`, and the half that makes it BITE is M5.62** -- `SubscriptionHub.publishSegment` now groups by `Subscriber` IDENTITY, so a consumer holding K runs of a segment is opened ONCE and handed the bytes ONCE while still receiving K pushes, each with its own run key, record count and first offset. ⚠️ **THE CONTRACT ANSWER, WHICH THIS ROW LEFT TO THE TAKER**: `Subscriber.open` takes the whole `List<Push>` and returns ONE sink, rather than one `open` per segment with pushes attached. It is the smaller change of the two the row named -- `Push` is untouched, and a consumer of one run receives a one-element list. ⚠️ **THE FAILURE ANSWER IS ALL-OR-NONE, AND IT IS FORCED RATHER THAN CHOSEN**: one sink is one byte stream, and a stream that threw part way through has delivered a PREFIX, so no run of it is whole and completing any would hand a consumer a fragment labelled as a segment. ⚠️ THE CASE THAT PINS IT FAILS EXACTLY ONCE, which is what makes it falsifiable: under the old contract this consumer's four runs had four sinks, so one throw killed one and the other THREE completed from a stream that had already broken -- measured, the case reds against the pre-change shape. A sink throwing on every write would fail all four under both contracts and pin nothing. ⚠️ **`SegmentDelivery`'s FAN-OUT MOVED WITH IT, AND ITS CONTRACT JAVADOC SAID THE OPPOSITE** -- the major review blocked on. `@param segmentFanOut` still read "a node subscribed to SEVERAL runs appears once per subscription ... M5.40 owns collapsing a node's subscriptions into one sink; until then this is an over-count", eight lines from the call site that now asserts otherwise. A taker wiring `direct` in M5.43 or M5.45b reads the only written description of the field, compensates for an inflation that no longer exists, and elects `DIRECT` for fan-outs `proxy` should serve -- one consumer-side GET per node where one proxy read served all. Corrected in the same commit, as M5.41 did for two javadocs. ⚠️ **AND IT CHANGES NO MODE TODAY, WHICH AN EARLIER DRAFT OF BOTH THIS CELL AND THE CODE COMMENT CLAIMED IT DID**: review measured that `modeFor` reads `segmentFanOut` only through `wantsDirect`, which first needs `servingPodUnderPressure \|\| !bytesInServingAz`, and this caller hardcodes `false` and `true` -- so the choice here turns on `batchBytes` alone. The number matters to the CONTRACT, not to the branch taken from that line. ⚠️ **GROUPED BY `==`, NOT `equals`**: a `Subscriber` is caller-supplied and may implement `equals` however it likes, so a plain `HashMap` key would let two subscribers that merely compare equal be merged -- handing one of them the other's stream. A linear scan would also not do: a segment carries ~1,600 runs and comparing each against every target built so far is quadratic, the same shape as a rate that scales with runs one layer in. ⚠️ **AND IT DOES NOT REACH NFR-5's 0.1%, WHICH IS WHY THE CRITERION IS PROPORTIONALITY**: one stream per consumer still sends the segment to each of ~9 nodes, ~6x ingested bytes cross-AZ, three orders over. The budget needs M5.16's per-AZ prefetch ⚠️ **THE TEST PASS FOUND THREE SURVIVING MUTATIONS AND ALL THREE ARE NOW KILLED.** (1) `complete(pushes.get(i))` -> `get(0)` survived: the `open` half of "these K pushes are yours" was pinned and the `complete` half was not pinned at all, because the two consumers in the first case hold the SAME runs so their lists are equal-valued. Disjoint run sets fix it. (2) The loop this commit added to `assembling` reduced to its first push survived, because every caller in the tree subscribes one `RunKey` per `Subscriber` so K is always 1 -- a node holding K runs would be told about run 0 and the rest would vanish silently. (3) Relaxing `ByIdentity` survived, and the MEASURED ANSWER CORRECTS THE FINDING: relaxing `equals` alone survives because `hashCode` is still `System.identityHashCode` and the two land in different buckets; relaxing `hashCode` alone survives because `==` then refuses the merge inside the bucket; only relaxing BOTH is catchable, and that is what the new case pins. So review's scenario -- a `record NodeSubscriber` collapsing under a generated `equals` -- does NOT reach. ⚠️ **TWO OF THE FIVE REDS ARE BOUND TO A MUTATION RATHER THAN TO THE PRE-CHANGE TREE**, stated because it is unusual: the equal-subscribers case and the per-consumer `complete` case both PASS against the old one-target-per-run shape, since without grouping nothing can merge and each consumer is completed per run anyway. They exist to pin new code against its own weakening, so their red is the weakening. ⚠️ **THREE MORE FALSE CLAIMS OF MINE WERE CUT**, all in prose written to explain the change: that the `LinkedHashMap` order was depended on by tests pairing sinks by index (review measured `HashMap` surviving -- the assertions use `containsExactlyInAnyOrder` and `deliver`'s by-index pairing is between its own parallel lists); that the failure case exercises a stream breaking PART WAY THROUGH (the policy answers `INLINE`, `writeHeldBytes` issues exactly ONE write of 17 bytes, so the fake throws on its only write with nothing delivered -- what it really discriminates is the number of SINKS the old contract opened); and that a linear scan would be "quadratic" (it is O(runs x consumers), quadratic only if consumers grew with runs) ⚠️ **ROUND 2 PASSED BOTH ROLES AND THE TEST PASS RETRACTED ITS OWN ROUND-1 FINDING**, having measured what this cell claims: `equals` alone survives, `hashCode` alone survives, both together are killed. ⚠️ **ONE GAP IS RECORDED RATHER THAN CLOSED**: `new SegmentDelivery(..., targets.size(), ...)` mutated to a constant 97 survives the whole suite, so nothing fails if the fan-out number regresses. It is not testable through the present seam -- `FetchPolicy` is final, `SegmentServing` is a record holding it, and `modeFor` reads `segmentFanOut` only through `wantsDirect`, which this call site makes unreachable by hardcoding its two conditions. M5.43 and M5.45b inherit it named. ⚠️ **AND A SITE ONE FILE OVER WAS STALE IN THE SAME WAY AS THE MAJOR**: `SegmentProxy`'s javadoc stated this very contract and then closed "Nothing enforces that here and no test can see it ... M5.40 carries it" -- false twice over now, since `OneStreamPerConsumerTest` does see it and M5.40 is split. Corrected to name what is still open, which is M5.40b and M5.62 |
| M5.40b | **ONE FETCH PER NODE ACROSS SEPARATE PUBLISHES**, which is cost.md R5 as `SegmentProxy`'s javadoc once claimed it. `streamTo` has no cache and coalesces nothing in flight, so every call is a fresh GET: one fan-out of 64 plus three LATE subscribers is four GETs for one object on one node, and a late subscriber is BY CONSTRUCTION not in a list already served -- M5's SPEC fetch-mode table names exactly that as a reason to serve `proxy`. Doc 10 §4's "AZ recovery: many nodes replaying the same backlog" is the same shape at scale. ⚠️ A CACHE IS WHAT CLOSES IT and a call-site rule is not: repeats across publishes cannot be merged by the caller, because the second publish has not happened when the first is served. SPEC rule R11 puts the cache in the ingester pods and M5.16 owns the per-AZ prefetch. ⚠️ WHOEVER TAKES IT DECIDES WHETHER THE CACHE SITS BEHIND THE STORE SEAM OR BESIDE IT -- `BinStore` is `SegmentProxy`'s only byte source today, so serving cached bytes means changing that -- and a cache is a new residency bound, so it owes an eviction rule and a memory ceiling with an assertion of its OWN. ⚠️ CRITERION 6 CANNOT SEE IT: it asks that service memory be FLAT IN K with a bound independent of K, and a cache of capacity C adds C at every K -- still flat, still K-independent -- so nothing that measures it distinguishes C = 8 MiB from C = 8 GiB. That includes `thePeakHandOffAndTheReadCountAreIDENTICALAtKONEAndKSIXTYFOUR`, which is one test OF the criterion rather than the criterion, and whose own javadoc says it is not evidence for criterion 6 alone | NFR-4 | ⚠️ **done** -- `SegmentCache` holds whole segments by key and `SegmentProxy.streamTo` consults it, so the row's own example is now measured: one fan-out of 64 plus three LATE subscribers is **ONE** GET, not four. ⚠️ **BESIDE THE STORE SEAM, NOT BEHIND IT**, which is the choice the row left open. A caching `BinStore` decorator would cache every GET in the process -- the commit log and the chain included, where a stale hit is a correctness bug rather than a saved request -- and `BinStore.get` returns an `InputStream`, so caching behind that seam means materialising inside the very seam that exists to avoid materialising. Beside it, the cache knows it holds whole segments and can bound itself in bytes. ⚠️ **THE EVICTION RULE IS LRU BY ACCESS, NOT BY INSERTION**, and that is not a detail: the segment every late subscriber is asking for is the one being read RIGHT NOW, so under insertion order it ages out while a segment nobody wants survives -- the exact case this row exists for. ⚠️ **TOO BIG IS REFUSED, NEVER ADMITTED THEN EVICTED.** Storing first would hold the oversized array for the width of that window, so the ceiling a reader was promised is exceeded by the one object most able to exhaust the heap. The proxy stops accumulating the moment the running total would cross the ceiling and streams the rest through uncached, so a segment larger than C is served normally and `bytesHeld()` never moves. ⚠️ **THE CEILING IS FOUR SEGMENTS AT THE CONFIGURED SIZE, VIA `SegmentCache.forSegmentsOf(config.maxSegmentBytes())`, AND THE FIRST VERSION HARDCODED 32 MiB -- A COST REGRESSION REVIEW MEASURED.** With a constant ceiling and `maxSegmentBytes` configured to 64 MiB, a size this project's own javadoc names, EVERY segment fails the oversized check, `put` returns silently, `bytesHeld()` reads zero forever and every repeat read is a fresh GET again -- no log, no metric, no error, every test green. That is precisely the silent regression M5.48 exists to make impossible, reintroduced one file over. FOUR is still CHOSEN AND NOT DERIVED, and the javadoc says so: it is enough that a late subscriber to any of the last few publishes hits, it is NOT computed from a heap budget, and it is the first number to revisit if serving-path residency turns out to matter. `bytesHeld()` is what a test or an operator reads to find out. ⚠️ **AND IT IS ON IN PRODUCTION**, with a case that fails if it is not -- `DefaultIngest` built `new SegmentProxy(store)`, whose ceiling is zero, and every cache test builds its own proxy, so reverting that one line would have left them all green while production issued a fresh GET for every repeat read. That is the M5.40a/M5.62 shape and this row does not repeat it. ⚠️ **A HIT IS STILL HANDED OVER A CHUNK AT A TIME**: writing the whole array in one call would make every consumer see a segment-sized slice, which is the shape `SegmentSink` exists to avoid, and would turn a hit into the buffer-then-forward ADR-0004 forbids. ⚠️ **A PREFIX IS NEVER CACHED**: admission happens only after the read completes, so a stream that threw part way leaves by the exception and no later subscriber is handed a fragment as a whole segment. ⚠️ **TWO GAPS, BOTH NAMED.** (1) IN-FLIGHT COALESCING IS NOT CLOSED and is **M5.63**: two publishes of the same segment that overlap in time both miss and both GET, because admission happens at the end of the read. The row's own example is sequential and this does not weaken it, but "coalesces nothing in flight" was in the problem statement and only half of it is answered here. (2) THE THREE-ARGUMENT `streamTo`, the per-chunk-deadline overload, has NO cache. It has no production caller -- M5.58 records that it must not be wired until the deadline path reports which sinks it dropped -- so the asymmetry costs nothing today and is stated so that whoever wires it does not inherit a silent one ⚠️ **ROUND 1 BLOCKED ON BOTH ROLES AND FOUND SIX REAL DEFECTS.** Beyond the ceiling regression: (1) the CLASS JAVADOC of the file this commit edits still said "this class holds no cache and coalesces nothing in flight ... one fan-out of 64 plus three late subscribers is four GETs ... M5.40 owns closing it", which this commit's own test now measures as ONE -- the same stale-contract shape M5.40a blocked on, this time in the edited file; (2) the same javadoc claimed the bytes materialised are `chunkBytes`, "independent of the SEGMENT SIZE and of K", false once an admission accumulates a segment -- independence from K survives, independence from segment size does not, and criterion 6's own cases build a capacity-0 proxy so they measure a configuration production no longer runs; (3) the ADVERTISED CEILING WAS NOT THE RESIDENCY BOUND -- a `ByteArrayOutputStream` doubles, so a 16 MiB segment held 16 and 32 MiB at once and then a third copy from `toByteArray`, ~67 MiB transient, while the javadoc told an operator to size their heap from `bytesHeld()`; growth is now clamped to the ceiling and the true peak is stated; (4) `writeChunked` COLLAPSED TO ONE SEGMENT-SIZED WRITE SURVIVED -- the miss path is pinned in `SegmentProxyTest` and the hit path, the one already holding the whole segment, had nothing; (5) three `>` to `>=` flips survived because no fixture sat on the boundary. ⚠️ **AND THE ALL-ZERO FIXTURE FINDING TOOK TWO GOES, WHICH IS WORTH KEEPING.** Review measured `new byte[4096]` making every content assertion vacuous. The first fix, `b[i] = i * 31 + 7`, was STILL vacuous and measurably so: that sequence has period 256, the chunk is 1024, and 31 x 1024 is 0 mod 256, so every chunk is byte-identical and repeating the first reproduces the array. Only a seeded `Random` kills the mutation. A fixture that looks varied is not the same as one that varies where the test slices it ⚠️ **ROUND 2 BLOCKED ON BOTH ROLES AGAIN, AND THE TWO MAJORS ARE THE SAME DEFECT SEEN FROM TWO SIDES: THE ROUND-1 FIXES WERE UNPINNED.** (1) The cost-regression fix -- deriving the ceiling from `config.maxSegmentBytes()` -- was invisible to the suite, because the only case reading the wired ceiling configured 8 MiB, which IS the default, and asserted `DEFAULT_CAPACITY_BYTES`: the trap's own value. Reverting to the hardcoded constant passed, and `forSegmentsOf` had no test caller at all. It now configures 16 MiB and asserts 64. (2) The TRIM was equally invisible: `cache.put(key, admitting)` WITHOUT trimming survived, because 4096 bytes at a 1024 chunk is the one size where the doubling ladder lands exactly, so `admitted == admitting.length` in every case and the trim was only ever taken in its no-op direction. In production a segment is any size, so an untrimmed accumulator carries TRAILING ZERO PADDING and every late subscriber is handed a segment with garbage appended, silently -- the precise opposite of what this row is for. The fixture is 4097 bytes now, and the odd byte is the whole point. ⚠️ **FOUR MORE FALSE CLAIMS OF MINE WERE CUT**, all in prose added to explain the round-1 fixes: a comment saying the cache was "built with the default ceiling" four lines above code that does the opposite; `forSegmentsOf` described as "the size THIS deployment is configured to write" when `maxSegmentBytes` is a flush TRIGGER, so a large `_bulk` body can exceed it and be served uncached; "A PREFIX IS NEVER CACHED", true only when the truncated read THROWS; and a thread-safety paragraph opening "NOT THREAD SAFE BY ITSELF" immediately before describing the lock that makes it so. ⚠️ **AND THE SILENT ARM OF THE PREFIX CLAIM IS A REAL GAP, OPENED AS M5.64**: a read that ends EARLY WITHOUT THROWING is admitted as a whole segment, and the cache turns what used to be one truncated delivery into a truncation served for as long as the entry lives ⚠️ **ROUND 3: THE TEST PASS PASSED, THE PRODUCTION PASS BLOCKED ON TWO MORE FALSE CLAIMS OF MINE, AND ITS MAJOR WAS IN THE ROW I HAD JUST OPENED.** M5.64 said the way out was that "the segment header carries its own length" -- conflating the HEADER's length with the SEGMENT's. `headerLen` exists so a reader can range-GET the preamble and directory; a segment is preamble + directory + DATA + footer and DATA is almost all of it, so checking bytes-read against `32 + headerLen` catches a truncation inside the tiny header and is BLIND to one in DATA, which is where a real one lands. A taker implements the cheap check, closes the row and ships the defect. The row now says what would actually close it -- the DIRECTORY carries each run's `byteStart` and `byteLen`, verified in `RunEntry` and `SegmentFormat`, so the expected total is derivable from a header this reader already holds. The minor was the same shape: "refused by `put`" names the wrong mechanism, since the proxy abandons its accumulator mid-loop and an oversized segment never reaches `put` at all. ⚠️ **AND THE TEST PASS, THOUGH IT PASSED, NAMED THREE GAPS WORTH CLOSING**: nothing pinned that the cache is keyed BY SEGMENT -- replacing both key arguments with a constant survived the whole tree, and the consequence is not a lost saving but consumers being handed another segment's bytes; the HIT path's dead-consumer drop was unpinned while the miss path's identical logic was covered; and `DEFAULT_SEGMENTS_HELD = 4 -> 1` survived because the assertion restated the constant on both sides. All three now have cases. ⚠️ **TEN FALSE CLAIMS ACROSS THREE FIX ROUNDS, AND NOT ONE NEW CODE DEFECT**: every round I introduced at least one false statement while explaining the previous round's fix, and none while writing the code. That is the pattern this row is worth remembering for |
| M5.41 | A SINK THAT BLOCKS STALLS THE WHOLE FAN-OUT, and `SegmentProxy` handles only a sink that THROWS. ⚠️ ROUND-1 REVIEW OF M5.12: the contract said "a slow or dead consumer must not stall" while only the dead half was implemented -- corrected in the javadoc, not in the code, because closing it is a design this row does not carry. ⚠️ THE FAILURE CHAIN IS CONCRETE: consumer 7 of 64 stalls on a zero TCP window and holds the serving thread, the shared chunk buffer and the open store `InputStream`; the store connection then idles past its read timeout and throws; `streamTo` propagates, and all 64 sinks are left holding a TRUNCATED PREFIX -- which a consumer cannot tell from a whole segment. That is the outcome `streamTo`'s own "the read is not abandoned" paragraph exists to prevent. ⚠️ IT MATTERS FROM M5.13/M5.14, not before: a blocking sink is the expected shape once these bytes go to a consumer's socket, and nothing wires one yet. ⚠️ WHAT IT NEEDS: a per-sink deadline and somewhere to put the slow ones -- and a decision about whether a slow consumer is dropped (it recovers from the log, like a dead one) or buffered (which reintroduces the per-consumer memory criterion 6 forbids) | NFR-6 | ⚠️ **done** -- `streamTo` gains a per-chunk deadline and drops any sink that misses it, so a sink that BLOCKS is handled exactly as one that THROWS. ⚠️ **THE DECISION THIS ROW ASKED FOR IS DROP, NOT BUFFER**, and the row's own text is the argument: buffering a slow consumer reintroduces per-consumer memory, which criterion 6 forbids by name, while dropping costs that consumer nothing it cannot recover -- it replays from the commit log exactly as a dead one does. A slow consumer and a dead consumer now get the same answer, which is also the simplest contract to state. ⚠️ **PER CHUNK, NOT PER SEGMENT**: a per-segment budget lets one consumer spend the whole of it and stall the others for its duration, which is the defect merely bounded. ⚠️ **ONE VIRTUAL THREAD PER SINK PER CHUNK** is what makes the deadline enforceable at all -- `SegmentSink.write` is a blocking call and nothing bounds it from outside without another thread. `invokeAll` takes the bound and does the waiting, so NO CLOCK IS READ and the method stays inside non-negotiable 7; timestamping around each write would have needed one. ⚠️ **THE BYTES STAY FLAT IN K** -- one shared buffer, no per-consumer copy -- and the TASK COUNT does not, which is stated in the javadoc rather than left for a reader to find. Criterion 6 measures the largest array the serving path materialises, and that is still the chunk. ⚠️ **A DROPPED SINK MAY STILL BE INSIDE `write` WHEN THE BUFFER IS REUSED**, recorded rather than overlooked: the array is refilled while a cancelled task may still be reading it, so a dropped consumer can be handed bytes that are neither its chunk nor a whole one. It is dropped, and `SubscriptionHub` completes only sinks that took every byte, so what it received is never presented as a segment. A private copy would be the per-consumer memory this path must not spend. ⚠️ **ROUND 1 FOUND THE FIRST FIX REINTRODUCING THE STALL FOUR LINES DOWN**, which is the finding this row turns on. The executor was closed with try-with-resources, and `ExecutorService.close()` is `shutdown()` plus an UNBOUNDED `awaitTermination` -- so the deadline dropped the slow sink from the fan-out and the call then waited for it anyway, holding the store `InputStream` open. Review MEASURED it: 202 ms to leave `invokeAll`, 4,011 ms to leave the try block, against a 200 ms deadline and a task that swallows interruption for 4 s. The executor is now shut down with `shutdownNow()` in a `finally` and NEVER awaited. ⚠️ **AND NO CASE COULD SEE IT**, because `BlockingSegmentSink` returns when interrupted and so cannot tell waiting from walking away. `UninterruptibleSegmentSink` can: it swallows the interrupt and reports whether it is still inside `write`, so the assertion is ORDERING rather than timing -- if `streamTo` returned while that sink had not left `write`, the serving path did not wait. It reds against the try-with-resources version. ⚠️ **ROUND 1 ALSO FALSIFIED THE CONTAINMENT ARGUMENT** licensing the deliberate buffer race: `SubscriptionHub.Tracking` marks a sink failed only from a CATCH, so a sink dropped for missing its deadline never throws, is never marked, and WOULD be handed `complete(...)`. The javadoc said the opposite. This overload is therefore NOT wired and must not be until the caller can learn WHICH sinks were dropped -- a count cannot say -- and that obligation is **M5.58**, opened here. ⚠️ IT HAD NO OWNER IN ROUND 2 and review caught that: M5.13, M5.14 and M5.41 are all done, so naming them left it with nobody -- the defect M5.44's row records. Two `src/main` javadocs that pointed a reader at M5.41 as the OPEN row are repointed in the same commit. ⚠️ **TWO CASES, AND EACH RED AGAINST ITS OWN PRE-STATE**: the drop case against the version that accepted the deadline and ignored it, the stall case against the try-with-resources version. ⚠️ **testing.md RULE 2 IS ALSO WHY THERE IS NO THIRD CASE**: `aSinkThatBLOCKSIsDroppedAndTheOthersAreServedWHOLE` carries both halves, because a separate "the read is not abandoned" case would have had NO RED RECORD -- the unfixed loop already delivered the whole segment to a live sink, it merely blocked first, so that assertion passed before and after. The count alone does not separate "dropped the slow one" from "failed the whole fan-out"; the survivors holding every byte is what does. ⚠️ **THE TWO-ARGUMENT `streamTo` IS UNCHANGED** and still has no deadline, so nothing that calls it today changes behaviour. Wiring the hub to the new overload is **M5.58's**, which is the precondition rather than the wiring. ⚠️ AN EARLIER DRAFT OF THIS SENTENCE NAMED M5.13, M5.14 AND M5.45b: the first two are DONE and the third is scoped to `direct`, so it assigned the work to nobody -- and it survived the round that fixed the same claim in two `src/main` javadocs, which is the restated-prose pattern exactly |

The row's own fixture reproduces exactly as predicted -- leader 1 commits `podb/i1` as `seg/0`, a DIFFERENT pod leads at K=1 and checkpoints, leader 3 takes the retry -- **2 deltas before, 1 after**. ⚠️ **FIX RATHER THAN PIN, AND THE DESIGN CALL IS ADR-COVERED**: the row warned that seeding the writer trades the recovery BOUND for coverage. A sink author who copies nothing on the strength of that promise gets a range that is neither its chunk nor a whole one. ⚠️ **DONE.** The three-argument `streamTo` returns `List<SegmentSink>` -- the sinks it dropped, in SUPPLY order, empty when every sink took every chunk.
